Numerical analysis of the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch equation with spin-torques
Agus L. Soenjaya

Abstract
. The current-induced magnetisation dynamics in a ferromagnet at elevated temperatures can be described by the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch (LLB) equation with spin-torque terms. In this paper, we focus on the regime above the Curie temperature. We first establish the existence and uniqueness of a global strong solution to the model in spatial dimensions d = 1 , 2 , 3 d=1,2,3 , under an additional smallness assumption on the initial data if d = 3 d=3 . Relevant smoothing and decay estimates are also derived. We then propose a fully discrete, linearly implicit finite element scheme for the problem and prove that it achieves optimal-order convergence, assuming adequate regularity of the exact solution. In addition, we introduce an unconditionally energy-stable finite element method for the case of negligible non-adiabatic torque. This scheme is also shown to converge optimally and, in the absence of current, preserves energy dissipation at the discrete level. Finally, we present numerical simulations that support the theoretical analysis and demonstrate the performance of the proposed methods.



1.
Introduction

The theory of micromagnetism is a widely used framework to analyse the behaviour of a ferromagnetic material on the micron scale. In contrast with the atomistic approach, continuum models in micromagnetics can be solved numerically for a much larger system. A standard model to describe the time evolution of magnetic configuration of a ferromagnetic body subject to some non-steady conditions is given by the Landau–Lifshitz equation
[
15
,
31
,
34
]
. However, numerous theoretical and experimental studies show that this model is not suitable at elevated temperatures since it cuts off all contributions from high frequency spin waves responsible for longitudinal magnetisation fluctuations
[
13
,
28
]
. This problem is especially crucial in applications since many modern magnetic devices such as the HAMR (heat-assisted magnetic recording) works at very high temperatures, often exceeding the so-called Curie temperature
[
42
,
53
,
56
]
.

A variant of the Landau–Lifshitz model widely used in the literature to describe the magnetisation dynamics at high temperatures is the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch (LLB) equation. This model takes into account longitudinal dynamics of the magnetisation and is valid below and above the critical temperature (the Curie temperature
T_{c}
)
[
6
,
14
,
25
]
. Let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d=1,2,3
) be a bounded domain. The general LLB equation describing the dynamics of magnetisation
\boldsymbol{u}:[0,T]\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is

(1.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}+\frac{\alpha_{1}}{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{H})\boldsymbol{u}-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}),

where
\gamma>0
is the gyromagnetic ratio,
\alpha_{1}>0
is the longitudinal damping coefficient, and
\alpha_{2}>0
is the transverse damping coefficient. This equation is equipped with an initial data and a homogeneous Neumann boundary condition. In (
1.1
), the effective field
\boldsymbol{H}:[0,T]\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the negative variational derivative of the micromagnetic energy functional
\mathcal{E}
to be specified later, i.e.
\boldsymbol{H}=-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{u}}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u})
. It is known that above the Curie temperature
[
6
,
14
]
, we have
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}:=\alpha
. Thus by means of the vector identity

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H})=(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{H})\boldsymbol{u}-|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{H},

equation (
1.1
) simplifies to

(1.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}+\alpha\boldsymbol{H}.

Here, we consider a standard micromagnetic energy functional

(1.3)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\underbrace{\frac{\sigma}{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}}_{\text{exchange}}+\underbrace{\frac{\kappa\mu}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}}_{\text{internal exchange}}+\underbrace{\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{\mathscr{D}}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}_{\text{anisotropy}},

which consists of the exchange energy, the internal exchange energy of Ginzburg–Landau type above the Curie temperature (so
\mu>0
), and the anisotropy energy
[
14
,
35
]
. This gives rise to an effective field

(1.4)

\boldsymbol{H}=\underbrace{\sigma\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}_{\text{exchange}}-\underbrace{\kappa\mu\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}_{\text{internal exchange}}-\underbrace{\lambda\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})}_{\text{anisotropy}},

where
\sigma>0
is the exchange damping constant,
\kappa=(2\chi)^{-1}
is a positive constant related to the longitudinal susceptibility of the material,
\mu>0
is a constant related to the equilibrium magnetisation,
\lambda
is the uniaxial anisotropy constant, and
\boldsymbol{e}
is a given unit vector denoting the anisotropy axis of the material. The constant
\lambda
in (
1.4
) determines the ease of magnetising in certain directions, which is positive in the presence of an ‘easy’ plane, or negative in the presence of an ‘easy’ axis. We assume
\lambda>0
in (
1.4
) for simplicity.
The analysis done in this paper will continue to hold if we add a time-varying applied magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}(t)
to
\boldsymbol{H}
, but for ease of presentation we take
\boldsymbol{B}(t)\equiv\boldsymbol{0}
. Note that with this choice of
\boldsymbol{H}
, the simplified problem (
1.2
) which holds above the Curie temperature is still a quasilinear vector-valued PDE.

In this paper, we focus on the LLB equation above
T_{c}
. More generally, we also include spin-torque terms due to currents
[
4
,
49
,
50
,
51
]
in our analysis, which are important for applications in spintronics
[
7
,
54
]
. Numerical analysis of the general LLB problem (
1.1
) will be addressed in a forthcoming paper.
All in all, for a given current density
\boldsymbol{\nu}:[0,T]\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the LLB equation with spin-torques above the Curie temperature reads:

(1.5a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}+\alpha\boldsymbol{H}+\beta_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}+\beta_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\,\qquad

for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\mathscr{D}
,

(1.5b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}=\sigma\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa\mu\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}-\lambda\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}),\qquad

for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\mathscr{D}
,

(1.5c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{u_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{D},

(1.5d)

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for }(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\partial\mathscr{D},

where
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are, respectively, the adiabatic and the non-adiabatic torques constants. Physical experiments
[
12
,
20
]
and theoretical studies
[
49
,
51
]
suggest that
|\beta_{2}|\ll|\beta_{1}|
, thus
\beta_{2}
is sometimes neglected in certain cases.
Note that a sufficiently regular solution of (
1.5
) satisfies an energy identity
[
13
]
. Indeed, formally taking the inner product of (
1.5a
) and (
1.5b
) with
\boldsymbol{H}
and
-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}
, respectively, gives

(1.6)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{H}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{H}\right\rangle+\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{H}\right\rangle,

(1.7)

\displaystyle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{H},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{\sigma}{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa\mu}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right).

Adding both equations above and integrating over
t\in(t_{1},t_{2})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t_{2})\big)+\alpha\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(1.8)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t_{1})\big)+\beta_{1}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}(s)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{H}(s)\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\beta_{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(s)\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}(s)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{H}(s)\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s,

where
\mathcal{E}
is the micromagnetic energy functional given in (
1.3
). If no current is present (
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
in (
1.8
)), then the system dissipates energy, i.e.
\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t_{2})\big)\leq\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t_{1})\big)
. We remark that the spin-torque terms cannot be written as a variational derivative of some energy functional
[
49
]
, thus adding complications to the analysis of the problem with these terms.

Existing results in the literature which are relevant to the present paper will be reviewed next. We remark that many variants of the standard Landau–Lifshitz equation attract significant interest in the mathematical literature, a non-exhaustive list includes
[
1
,
17
,
24
,
41
,
44
,
48
]
, which take into account various effects not included in the standard model. Specifically for the LLB equation, a rigorous analysis of the model (without spin-torques) is initiated in
[
35
]
, where the existence of a weak solution is obtained. The existence, uniqueness, and decay of the strong solution are discussed in
[
36
]
. These papers, however, do not take into account the spin-torque terms in the analysis. The existence of a weak solution (but without uniqueness in the general case) for the LLB equation with spin-torques is shown in
[
7
]
, but the existence of a strong solution is not known yet. Spin-torque effects have also been considered for the Landau–Lifshitz–Gilbert equation
[
52
]
, where a local-in-time strong solution is obtained in that case.

On the aspect of numerical analysis, several fully discrete finite element methods to approximate the solution of the LLB equation without spin-torques are proposed in
[
36
]
and
[
47
]
. However, numerical schemes developed in these papers actually approximate the
regularised
version of the equation,
not
the LLB equation itself. The strong solution of the regularised problem is shown to converge to that of (
1.5
) as the regularisation parameter goes to zero. As such, the order of convergence of the numerical scheme is also dependent on this parameter, which theoretically blow up as the regularisation parameter vanishes. Similar approach using regularisation is also taken in the stochastic case
[
27
]
, where a suboptimal order of convergence in probability is obtained for
d=1
and
2
. In
[
9
]
, a finite element scheme is proposed to approximate the LLB equation without spin-torques directly, but convergence is only shown conditionally along a subsequence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
without rate, while energy dissipativity at the discrete level is not guaranteed by the scheme. Moreover, this scheme is only conditionally stable and is nonlinear, in that a system of nonlinear equations needs to be solved at each time step. In any case, numerical discretisation of the LLB equation with spin-torques (
1.5
) has not been explored yet in the literature.

This paper aims to address the aforementioned gaps. Our primary objectives are to establish the global well-posedness of (
1.5
) and to develop a numerical scheme that converges unconditionally in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
at a provably optimal rate, without requiring regularisation. To this end, we first show the unique existence of global strong solution to (
1.5
) for
d=1,2,3
. We then propose a fully discrete, linearly implicit scheme which applies directly to (
1.5
) without the need for regularisation. This scheme is stable and convergent in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap\ell^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
. However, due to difficulties arising from nonlinear cross-product terms, we are unable to prove convergence – or even stability – in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
for this scheme. To achieve provably unconditional convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
, we are led to consider a second fully discrete scheme to solve (
1.5
). For the case
\beta_{2}=0
, corresponding to negligible non-adiabatic torque (which is a valid approximation for transition-metal ferromagnets dominated by exchange fields
[
4
,
51
]
), we are able to prove unconditional stability and convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
for this scheme at an optimal rate. Additionally, this second scheme preserves energy dissipation at the discrete level in the absence of spin current (
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
), a desirable feature consistent with the underlying physical model.

As far as we know, this is the first time a linear scheme is proposed to directly approximate the LLB equation (with or without spin-torques) above the Curie temperature, for which unconditional convergence with an optimal rate is shown rigorously. An attempt to maintain energy dissipativity of the LLB equation without spin-torques at the discrete level, while still attaining convergence at the optimal rate, is also addressed for the first time by our second numerical scheme. Some numerical experiments to corroborate the analysis are also provided.
To summarise, our main contributions in this paper are:

(1)

Proving the existence and uniqueness of global strong solution to the LLB equation with spin-torques, unconditionally when
d=1
or
2
, and for small initial data when
d=3
, which build on the results in
[
7
,
35
]
. Physically relevant smoothing and decay estimates are also provided (cf. Theorem
3.8
and
3.9
).

(2)

Proposing a fully discrete linearly implicit finite element scheme to approximate (
1.5
) with an unconditionally optimal order of convergence, namely with a spatial rate of
r+1
and a temporal rate of
1
in the
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap\ell^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
norm for a finite element space of degree
r
(cf. Theorem
4.9
). This removes the need for regularisation in
[
36
]
, while also taking spin-torque terms into account.

(3)

Proposing a fully discrete (but nonlinear) finite element scheme to approximate the LLB equation for the case
\beta_{2}=0
, which maintains energy dissipativity at the discrete level in the absence of spin current. An optimal order of convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
is also shown unconditionally (cf. Theorem
5.11
), improving the results of
[
9
]
in several aspects.

The existence of a global strong solution is shown in Section
3
by deriving further a priori estimates in stronger norms. These estimates, in turn, depend on a uniform bound in the
\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
-norm, which – unlike in the case of the standard Landau–Lifshitz equation – is nontrivial to obtain due to the lack of magnetisation length conservation and the added complexity introduced by the spin-torque terms. We then proceed to propose some finite element schemes. In Section
4
, by using an elliptic projection adapted to the problem at hand, we show an optimal order of convergence for the linear finite element scheme to approximate (
1.5
). In Section
5
, we devise a nonlinear scheme (for the equation (
1.5
) with
\beta_{2}=0
) that converges unconditionally in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
and preserves energy dissipativity in the absence of spin current. A detailed analysis is carried out to establish the claimed optimal order of convergence. This analysis relies on a stability estimate in
\ell^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{\infty})
, which, in our setting, is again not immediate. Several numerical simulations are presented in Section
6
to support the theoretical results and to compare the two proposed schemes. Finally, Section
7
offers concluding remarks and outlines potential directions for future research. The discussion of a fixed-point iteration method for solving the nonlinear scheme at each time step is deferred to Appendix
A
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

We begin by defining some notations used in this paper. Let
\mathscr{D}
be a bounded open domain. The function space
\mathbb{L}^{p}:=\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{3})
denotes the usual space of
p
-th integrable functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbb{W}^{k,p}:=\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{3})
denotes the usual Sobolev space of
functions on
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The function space
\mathcal{C}^{0}:=\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{3})
is the space of continuous functions on
\mathscr{D}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We
write
\mathbb{H}^{k}:=\mathbb{W}^{k,2}
. The Laplacian operator acting on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
-valued functions is denoted by
\Delta
with domain
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}:=\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\mathscr{D})
given by

(2.1)

\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}:\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ on }\partial\mathscr{D}\right\}.

If
X
is a Banach space, the spaces
L^{p}(0,T;X)
and
W^{k,p}(0,T;X)
denote respectively the usual Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces of functions on
(0,T)
taking values in
X
. The space
C([0,T];X)
denotes the space of continuous functions on
[0,T]
taking values in
X
. For simplicity, we will write
L^{p}(\mathbb{W}^{m,r}):=L^{p}(0,T;\mathbb{W}^{m,r})
and
L^{p}(\mathbb{L}^{q}):=L^{p}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{q})
. The norm in a Banach space
X
is denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{X}
. We denote the scalar product in a Hilbert space
H
by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{H}
and its corresponding norm by
\|\cdot\|_{H}
. We will not distinguish between the scalar product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
vector-valued functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the scalar product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
matrix-valued functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
, and denote them by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.

Finally, the constant
C
in the estimates denotes a generic constant which may take different values at different occurrences. If
the dependence of
C
on some variable, e.g.
T
, is highlighted, we will write

C(T)
.

2.2.
Formulations and assumptions

Here, we formulate the problem more precisely and state several assumptions which will be used throughout the paper. For simplicity of presentation and to study the influence of the damping constant and spin-torques in the analysis, we only keep the coefficients
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
in the equation, and set the rest of the coefficients, namely
\gamma,\sigma,\kappa,\mu,\lambda
to
1
in (
1.5
), unless otherwise specified. We assume that
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is a given vector field such that
\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{4}(\mathscr{D};\,\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq\nu_{\infty}
, where
\boldsymbol{n}
is the outward pointing normal vector and
\nu_{\infty}
is a positive constant. Without loss of generality, we assume the damping parameter
\alpha<1
in the estimates.

Definition 2.1
.

Given
T>0
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
, a
strong solution
to (
1.5
) is a function

\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{2})

such that
\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, and that for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\big(\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{w}(t)\big),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\big(\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{w}(t)\big),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

(2.2)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle,

where for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}(t)=\boldsymbol{u}(t)+|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\boldsymbol{e}\big(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big).

In this case, (
1.5
) is satisfied for almost every
(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathscr{D}
.

At times, we write
\boldsymbol{H}:=\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}
, where
\boldsymbol{w}
in (
2.3
) represents the remaining terms in the effective field.
Note that by the vector identity (
2.9
), we can write

(2.4)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})-\beta_{1}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{u},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})-\beta_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\times(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{u},

where the last term on the right-hand side of (
2.5
) is a zero vector.
Thus, if we assume that
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is tangential to the boundary (
\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
, which is physically reasonable), then by (
2.4
), (
2.5
), and the divergence theorem,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\beta_{1}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}\left\langle(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(t))\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

(2.7)

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}(t),\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\boldsymbol{\chi})\right\rangle.

Therefore, integrating by parts and applying the above identities, we can also write (
2.2
) as

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}(t)),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\alpha\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

(2.8)

\displaystyle\quad-\beta_{1}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}\left\langle(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{\chi})\right\rangle,

for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
, which is the weak formulation that we will use in Section
4
.

2.3.
Auxiliary results

Our analysis requires several auxiliary results. We begin by recalling the following vector identities: For all
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle=(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{b})\boldsymbol{a}+(\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{a},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\nabla\left(|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{b}\right)

\displaystyle=2\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{a})+|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{b},

(2.11)

\displaystyle 2\boldsymbol{a}\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{b}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{b}|^{2},

(2.12)

\displaystyle 4|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{a}\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{a}|^{4}-|\boldsymbol{b}|^{4}+\left(|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{b}|^{2}\right)^{2}+2|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}|\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{b}|^{2}.

Next, we state the following elliptic regularity result.

Lemma 2.2

(Elliptic regularity)
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a bounded domain with
C^{2}
-smooth boundary or a convex polyhedral domain. There exists a constant
C:=C(\mathscr{D})
such that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, where
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
is defined in (
2.1
), we have

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left\|{D^{2}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right),

where
D^{2}\boldsymbol{v}
is the Hessian of
\boldsymbol{v}
.

More generally, let
\mathscr{D}
be a bounded domain with
C^{k+2}
-smooth boundary, where
k
is a non-negative integer. There exists a constant
C:=C(k,\mathscr{D})
such that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{k+2}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k+2}}\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k}}\right).

Proof.

See
[
29
, Chapter 2–3]
.
∎

Some frequently used interpolation inequalities are collected in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given and
d\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then there exists a positive constant
C
such that the following inequalities hold.

(i)

There exists a constant
C:=C(\mathscr{D})
such that for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{\frac{d}{6}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.17)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{\frac{d}{4}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

(ii)

Let

(2.18)

p\in\begin{cases}[1,\infty],&\text{ if $d=1$},\\
[1,\infty),&\text{ if $d=2$},\\
[1,6],&\text{ if $d=3$}.\end{cases}

There exists a constant
C:=C(\mathscr{D},p)
such that for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, we have

(2.19)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

If, furthermore,
\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
, then we also have

(2.20)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(iii)

For any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{3}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, we have

(2.21)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right).

(iv)

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a convex polyhedral domain. There exists a constant
C:=C(\mathscr{D},q)
such that for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
satisfying
\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{q}
for some
q>d
, we have

(2.22)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{q}}.

Proof.

Estimates (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) follow from the Gagliardo–Nirenberg and the Young inequalities.

Next, we show (
2.19
). Let
\{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
be a complete countable set in
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
of orthonormal eigenfunctions of the negative Neumann Laplacian
-\Delta
, with corresponding eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
. The eigenvalues can be arranged so that
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{3}\leq\ldots
, with
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}=|\mathscr{D}|^{-1}
. Therefore, for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, we can write
\Delta\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}
, where
a_{k}=\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}\right\rangle
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{0}=0
. This means
\boldsymbol{v}=b_{0}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\lambda_{k}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}
, where
b_{0}:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\right\rangle
. We then have

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}=-\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}^{2}\lambda_{k}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{1}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}^{2}=\lambda_{1}^{-1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

thus showing (
2.19
) for
p=2
. For
p\in[1,2)
, we have
\mathbb{L}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{p}
since
\mathscr{D}
is bounded. For
p\in(2,p^{\ast}]
, where
p^{\ast}
is any number (or
\infty
) such that the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{p^{\ast}}
holds, we have by this embedding,

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{D^{2}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where we used (
2.13
) and (
2.19
) for
p=2
proven earlier. This proves (
2.19
) for all
p
satisfying (
2.18
).

Similarly, integrating by parts we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}=-\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where in the last step we used (
2.19
). This implies (
2.20
) for
p=2
. For
p\in(2,p^{\ast}]
, with
p^{\ast}
as defined above, by Sobolev embedding we then have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

thus showing (
2.20
) for all
p
satisfying (
2.18
).

Inequality (
2.21
) follows from (
2.21
) as

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\right)\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right),

where in the last step we used (
2.20
).

Finally, regularity estimate (
2.22
) is a result of
[
40
]
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We also state the following well-known (continuous and discrete) Gronwall-type inequalities for ease of reference later.

Lemma 2.4

(Gronwall’s lemma)
.

Let
\eta(\cdot)
be a non-negative absolutely continuous function on
[0,T]
, which satisfies for a.e.
t
the differential inequality

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(t)\leq\phi(t)\eta(t)+\psi(t),

where
\phi(\cdot)
and
\psi(\cdot)
are non-negative integrable function on
[0,T]
. Then

\displaystyle\eta(t)\leq\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\left[\eta(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\psi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

See
[
23
, Appendix B.2]
.
∎

Lemma 2.5

(Discrete Gronwall’s lemma)
.

Let
k,B,a_{j},b_{j}
, and
\gamma_{j}
be non-negative numbers (for all integers
j\geq 0
) such that

\displaystyle a_{n}+k\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{j}\leq B+k\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}a_{j}.

Then

\displaystyle a_{n}+k\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{j}\leq B\exp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}\right).

Proof.

Set
u_{0}=B
, and for any
m\geq 1
let
u_{m}:=B+k\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\gamma_{j}a_{j}
. Then for any
m\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle u_{m}=u_{m-1}+\gamma_{m-1}a_{m-1}\leq(1+\gamma_{m-1})u_{m-1}\leq e^{\gamma_{m-1}}u_{m-1}.

Noting the assumption, this implies

\displaystyle a_{n}+k\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{j}\leq B+k\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}a_{j}=u_{n}\leq u_{0}\exp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}\right)=B\exp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}\right),

as required.
∎

Lemma 2.6

(Generalised discrete Gronwall’s lemma)
.

Let
k,B,a_{j},b_{j},c_{j}
, and
\gamma_{j}
be non-negative numbers (for all integers
j\geq 0
) such that

\displaystyle a_{n}+k\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{j}\leq k\sum_{j=0}^{n}\gamma_{j}a_{j}+k\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{j}+B.

Suppose that
k\gamma_{j}<1
for all
j\geq 0
, and set
\sigma_{j}:=(1-k\gamma_{j})^{-1}
. Then

\displaystyle a_{n}+k\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{j}\leq\exp\left(k\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sigma_{j}\gamma_{j}\right)\left[k\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{j}+B\right].

Proof.

See
[
32
, Lemma 5.1]
.
∎

3.
Existence and uniqueness of strong solution

To show the global existence and uniqueness of solution to the problem (
1.5
), one could apply the Faedo–Galerkin method and establish appropriate uniform estimates on the approximate solution. In order to simplify presentation, we will work directly with the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
(instead of its approximation). These a priori estimates can be made rigorous by working with the Galerkin approximation as in
[
7
,
35
]
.

In this section, we assume that
\mathscr{D}
is a bounded open domain with smooth boundary, so that relevant elliptic regularity results can be used. Existence and uniqueness of global strong solution still hold if
\mathscr{D}
is a convex polyhedral domain (see Remark
3.10
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a solution of (
1.5
) and let
p\in[2,\infty)
. For all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha p\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(s)|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}p\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{p}(1+t),

where
C_{p}
is a constant depending on
p
,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}
, and the coefficients of (
1.5
). Furthermore,

(3.2)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\sqrt{\beta},

where
\beta:=(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}\alpha^{-1}
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of (
1.5a
) with
|\boldsymbol{u}|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u}
, integrating by parts, and noting that
(\boldsymbol{a}\times\boldsymbol{b})\cdot\boldsymbol{a}=0
for any
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\alpha\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla(\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u})\right\rangle+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}=\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u},|\boldsymbol{u}|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle.

Using the identity

\displaystyle\nabla\left(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{q}\boldsymbol{u}\right)=|\boldsymbol{u}|^{q}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+q|\boldsymbol{u}|^{q-2}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}),\quad\forall q\geq 0,

to expand the second term on the left-hand side,
we have by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\alpha\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha(p-2)\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-4}{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p}{2}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}}{\alpha}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\frac{\alpha}{4}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}.

After rearranging the terms, we have

(3.4)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\frac{\alpha p}{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\beta p\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}-p\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2},

where
\beta=(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}\alpha^{-1}
.
By considering the maximum value of the function
x\mapsto\beta x^{p}-x^{p+2}
for
p\geq 2
, we have the inequality

\displaystyle\beta\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}\leq\left(\frac{2\beta|\mathscr{D}|}{p+2}\right)\left(\frac{\beta p}{p+2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}.

Substituting this to the right-hand side of (
3.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\frac{\alpha p}{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\left(\frac{2\beta|\mathscr{D}|p}{p+2}\right)\left(\frac{\beta p}{p+2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\leq 2\beta^{1+\frac{p}{2}}|\mathscr{D}|.

Integrating this over
(0,t)
, we obtain (
3.1
). In particular, rearranging the terms and taking the
p
-th root, we have

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}+\left(2|\mathscr{D}|\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\beta^{\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2}}.

Letting
p\to\infty
then yield (
3.2
). This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

If
\beta
in Proposition
3.1
is sufficiently small such that
\beta<\mu
, then we can absorb all the terms on the right-hand side of (
3.3
) to the left, leading to an exponential decay estimate

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq e^{-(\mu-\beta)t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}},

similar to that in
[
36
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a solution of (
1.5
). For all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\alpha^{-2}(1+t),

where
C
is a constant depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
and the coefficients of (
1.5
).

Proof.

Taking the inner product of (
1.5a
) with
-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}
and integrating by parts, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u},\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u},\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

(3.6)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

We estimate each term by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities as follows:

\displaystyle\left|{I_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\alpha^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\alpha^{-1}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\alpha^{-1}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

We substitute this back into (
3.6
), rearrange the terms, and integrate over
(0,t)
. Discarding some non-negative terms on the left-hand side and noting the assumption
\alpha<1
(so that
\alpha^{-1}\leq\alpha^{-2}
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\alpha^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\alpha^{-2}(1+t),

where the last estimate is obtained by applying (
3.1
) with
p=2
and
4
. This proves (
3.5
).
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a solution of (
1.5
). If
d=1
or
2
, then for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ce^{C\alpha^{-8}t^{3}},

where
C
is a constant depending on the coefficients of (
1.5
) and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
.

If
d=3
, then inequality (
3.7
) holds under an additional assumption that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\beta\lesssim\alpha
, where
\beta
was defined in Proposition
3.1
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of (
1.5a
) with
\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
and integrating by parts as necessary, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\nabla\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\beta_{1}\left\langle\nabla\big((\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}\left\langle\nabla\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

(3.8)

\displaystyle=:J_{1}+J_{2}+\cdots+J_{5}.

The term
J_{1}
will be handled last. For the term
J_{2}
, noting (
3.2
) and (
3.5
), by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities as well as (
3.2
) we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-3}(1+t)+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{3}
, by Young’s inequality, (
2.10
), and (
3.2
) we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha\left\|{\nabla(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-1}(1+t)+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{4}
, by Hölder’s and Young’s inequality, (
3.1
), as well as (
2.13
) and (
2.19
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the last term, we expand the gradient term, then use the Hölder and the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality
\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}^{2}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
, as well as elliptic regularity (
2.14
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-1}(1+t)^{2}+C\alpha^{-1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used elliptic regularity (
2.14
) and Proposition
3.1
.
It remains to estimate
J_{1}
. To this end, we will consider each case
d=1,2
, or
3
separately.

Case 1 (
d=1
):
By Hölder’s and Agmon’s inequalities (noting (
3.5
)), we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\alpha^{-5}(1+t)^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Case 2 (
d=2
):
By the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality (
2.17
) and (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq C\alpha^{-5}(1+t)^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Case 3 (
d=3
):

We note the following Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities in 3D:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}

\displaystyle\leq B_{\mathrm{GL}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{\frac{2}{3}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{\frac{1}{3}}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{GL}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{3}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{\frac{2}{3}},

where
B_{\mathrm{GL}}
and
C_{\mathrm{GL}}
are constants depending on
\mathscr{D}
.
By (
3.9
), (
3.10
), (
3.2
), and (
2.21
) we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq B_{\mathrm{GL}}C_{\mathrm{GL}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq B_{\mathrm{GL}}C_{\mathrm{GL}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\sqrt{\beta}\right)\big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.11)

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-1}\left(1+t\right)+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+B_{\mathrm{GL}}C_{\mathrm{GL}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\sqrt{\beta}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we also used (
3.1
) and Young’s inequality, while
\beta
was defined in Proposition
3.1
.

We now collect all the above estimates and substitute them back to (
3.8
). If
d=1
or
2
, we have

(3.12)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\alpha^{-5}(1+t)^{2}\left(1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right).

Therefore, by the Gronwall lemma (Lemma
2.4
), we obtain

(3.13)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(C\alpha^{-5}(1+t)^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\left[\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\alpha^{-5}(1+t^{3})\right]\leq Ce^{C\alpha^{-8}t^{3}},

where
C
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
, and in the last step we also used (
3.5
).

On the other hand, for
d=3
, if

(3.14)

B_{\mathrm{GL}}C_{\mathrm{GL}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\sqrt{\beta}\right)\leq\alpha/8,

then we can absorb the term containing
\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}
in (
3.11
). Integrating (
3.12
) over
(0,t)
and using (
3.13
) again, we infer (
3.7
).
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Proposition
3.4
implies the existence of a global solution
\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{4})
for any initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
for
d=1
or
2
. For
d=3
, this holds under an additional assumption that
\alpha
is sufficiently large, or
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\sqrt{\beta}
is sufficiently small (see (
3.14
)). If the coefficients of (
1.5
) satisfy the assumptions described in Remark
3.2
, then
\beta
can be taken to be zero.
Note that a
local
solution with the aforementioned regularity exists by similar argument as in
[
36
]
.

From this point onwards, for ease of presentation we will not track the dependence of the estimates on
\alpha
,
\beta_{1}
, or
\beta_{2}
anymore since they will not be essential for the argument. Previously, we track this dependence to ensure it does not interfere with the smallness condition on the initial data assumed in (
3.14
).

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a solution of (
1.5
) such that Proposition
3.4
holds. For all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.15)

\displaystyle t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}s\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}},

where
C
is a constant depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
and the coefficients of (
1.5
).

Proof.

Applying the operator
-\Delta
on (
1.5a
), then taking the inner product of the result with
t\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
and integrating by parts as necessary, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\alpha t\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha t\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-2t\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-t\left\langle\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha t\left\langle\Delta(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}t\left\langle\Delta\big((\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}t\left\langle\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

(3.16)

\displaystyle=:J_{1}+J_{2}+\cdots+J_{6}.

We will estimate each term on the last line, noting the estimates (
3.5
) and (
3.7
) obtained previously. The first term is left as is. To estimate
J_{2}
, we first note that by the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality (
2.16
) and elliptic regularity we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\leq C\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{6}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{\frac{d}{6}}\leq C\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{6}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{d}{6}},

where
d\leq 3
is the spatial dimension.
Therefore, we infer by (
2.19
) and Young’s inequality (with exponents
p=\frac{12}{6-d}
and
q=\frac{12}{6+d}
) that

\displaystyle\left|{J_{2}}\right|\leq 2t\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{6}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1+\frac{d}{6}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{12}{6-d}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha t}{10}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

\displaystyle\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha t}{10}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used (
3.7
).
For the term
J_{3}
, using the fact that
\mathbb{H}^{2}
is an algebra for
d\leq 3
, by elliptic regularity (
2.14
), inequalities (
3.1
) and (
3.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}+\frac{\alpha t}{10}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Similarly, for the term
J_{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{3}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}+\frac{\alpha t}{10}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the last two terms, by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, (
3.1
) and (
3.7
), as well as elliptic regularity estimates (
2.14
) and (
2.21
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{\alpha t}{10}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{J_{6}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{\alpha t}{10}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, from (
3.16
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\alpha t\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right).

Integrating both sides over
(0,t)
and applying (
3.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}s\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}Cse^{Cs^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}Cse^{Cs^{3}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}}+Cte^{Ct^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+Ct^{2}e^{Ct^{3}}\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}},

where in the last step we used an elementary inequality
1+t+t^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}}
for all
t\geq 0
and (
3.7
) again. This proves the required estimate.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a solution of (
1.5
) such that Proposition
3.4
holds. For all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.17)

\displaystyle t^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}},

where
C
is a constant depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
and the coefficients of (
1.5
).

Proof.

Applying the operator
\Delta^{2}
on (
1.5a
), then taking the inner product of the result with
t^{2}\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
and integrating by parts as necessary, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\alpha t^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha t^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha t^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=t\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t^{2}\left\langle\nabla\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-t^{2}\left\langle\nabla\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\big),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha t^{2}\left\langle\nabla\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}t^{2}\left\langle\Delta^{2}\big((\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}t^{2}\left\langle\nabla\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

(3.18)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{6}.

We will estimate each term on the last line. In simplifying the estimates, we often use an elementary inequality
1+t+t^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}}
. Now, the first term in (
3.18
) is kept as is. For the term
I_{2}
, using the fact that
\mathbb{H}^{2}
is an algebra for
d\leq 3
, Hölder’s inequality, the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, and elliptic regularity estimates (
2.14
) and (
2.21
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=t^{2}\left|{\left\langle\Delta(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle+\left\langle\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=t^{2}\left|{\left\langle\Delta(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle+\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle+\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla^{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{2}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla^{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{2}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right)\left(\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ce^{Ct^{3}}+\frac{\alpha t^{2}}{10}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality, (
3.1
), (
3.7
), and (
3.15
). For the term
I_{3}
, we argue in a similar manner to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|\leq Ct^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}}+\frac{\alpha t^{2}}{10}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
I_{4}
, noting the identity (
2.10
), using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, the fact that
\mathbb{H}^{2}
is an algebra, and elliptic regularity estimates (
2.14
) and (
2.21
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle=\alpha t^{2}\left|{2\left\langle\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u})\big),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle+\left\langle\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{\alpha t^{2}}{10}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}+\frac{\alpha t^{2}}{10}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used (
3.1
), (
3.7
), and (
3.15
). For the term
I_{5}
, it is clear that we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha t^{2}}{10}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the last term, we apply similar argument as in the estimate for
I_{2}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{I_{6}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ce^{Ct^{3}}+\frac{\alpha t^{2}}{10}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, we substitute these estimates into (
3.18
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\alpha t^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2t\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Cte^{Ct^{3}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ce^{Ct^{3}}.

Integrating over
(0,t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle t^{2}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}Cs\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}Cse^{Cs^{3}}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}Ce^{Cs^{3}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}}+Ce^{Ct^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}s\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}Ce^{Cs^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}},

where in the last step we used (
3.15
).
This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.8
.

Let the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
be given, with an additional condition that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}
is sufficiently small (as given by Proposition
3.4
) if
d=3
. Then there exists a unique global strong solution
\boldsymbol{u}
to (
1.5
) in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

Furthermore, this solution satisfies

(3.19)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct^{3}}(1+t^{-2}),

where
C
is a constant depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
and the coefficients of (
1.5
).

Proof.

The existence of a global strong solution follows from a standard compactness argument and the Aubin–Lions lemma, making use of the uniform a priori estimates in (
3.2
), (
3.5
), and (
3.7
). Inequality (
3.19
) follows from (
3.2
), (
3.5
), (
3.7
), (
3.15
), and (
3.17
).

Finally, we show the uniqueness of strong solution by deriving a continuous dependence estimate. Let
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
be strong solutions of (
1.5
) corresponding to initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
, respectively. Let
\boldsymbol{w}:=\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
. Then for almost every
(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathscr{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{w}

\displaystyle=\alpha\Delta\boldsymbol{w}-\alpha\boldsymbol{w}-\alpha\left(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}+((\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}\right)-\alpha\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})-\left(\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{w}+\boldsymbol{w}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})+\boldsymbol{w}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\right)+\beta_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{w}+\beta_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{w}+\boldsymbol{w}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}\right).

Taking the inner product of this equation with
\boldsymbol{w}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\boldsymbol{w}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle

(3.20)

\displaystyle=:J_{1}+J_{2}+\cdots+J_{5}.

Straightforward applications of Young’s inequality and Sobolev embedding yields

\displaystyle\left|{J_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\boldsymbol{w}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{J_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\left|{J_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{J_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

For the term
J_{2}
, by Hölder’s inequality, (
2.22
), and (
2.20
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{2}}\right|\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

We substitute the above estimates into (
3.20
) and use elliptic regularity to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

By the Gronwall lemma (Lemma
2.4
), noting (
3.1
), (
3.5
), and (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(0)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\exp\left(Ce^{Ct^{3}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Uniqueness of the solution then follows immediately from this stability estimate.
∎

Physical theory predicts a loss of magnetisation in a ferromagnet above the Curie temperature, the rate of which is important in the study of ultrafast demagnetisation phenomena at elevated temperatures
[
2
,
13
,
33
]
. We end this section with the following result, which shows precisely the asymptotic behaviour of the magnetisation
\boldsymbol{u}
in the absence of current (
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
) above the Curie temperature, as predicted by the physical theory. We keep the coefficients of the equation as in (
1.5
) for this proposition to obtain a precise statement on the rate of decay. Inequality (
3.21
) has been shown in
[
36
]
, but we provide the proof here in our context for ease of reference. Inequality (
3.22
) and its consequence is new.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
and let
\mathcal{E}
be the energy functional defined in (
1.3
). Suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}
is a strong solution to (
1.5
) corresponding to an initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Then we have

(3.21)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\kappa\mu t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha\kappa\mu t}\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\big).

In particular,
\boldsymbol{u}(t)\to\boldsymbol{0}
uniformly in space and
\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big)\to 0
(thus
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\to 0
as well) as
t\to\infty
. This implies that as time progresses, the magnetisation vectors will align with each other with magnitude decaying to zero.

Proof.

As in the proof of (
3.1
) but with
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, taking the inner product of (
1.5a
) with
|\boldsymbol{u}|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u}
and integrating by parts, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\alpha\sigma\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla(\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u})\right\rangle+\alpha\kappa\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\alpha\kappa\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}+\alpha\lambda\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\alpha\sigma\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\sigma(p-2)\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-4}{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha\kappa\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\alpha\kappa\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}+\alpha\lambda\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}=0,

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+p\alpha\kappa\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}\leq 0,

so that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(e^{p\alpha\kappa\mu}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}\right)\leq 0.

Integrating this over
(0,t)
and taking
p
-th root give

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq e^{-\alpha\kappa\mu t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}.

Letting
p\to\infty
shows (
3.21
).

Next, we show (
3.22
). The same argument as (
1.6
) and (
1.7
) but with
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
yields

(3.23)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{\sigma}{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa\mu}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}=0.

On the other hand, noting (
1.5b
) we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sigma\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa\mu\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}-\lambda\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}),\sigma\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa\mu\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}-\lambda\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa\mu\sigma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa\sigma\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+4\kappa\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\mu^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa^{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{6}+2\kappa\mu\lambda\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\lambda\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa\lambda\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\lambda^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where we expanded the inner product and used integration by parts and (
2.10
) as necessary in the last step. Substituting (
3.24
) into (
3.23
), noting the definition of
\mathcal{E}
in (
1.3
), and discarding some non-negative terms on the left-hand side, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u})+2\alpha\kappa\mu\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u})\leq 0,

which implies (
3.22
).

Letting
t\to\infty
in (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) shows the last statement in the proposition. As time advances, the magnetisation vectors will align with each other (approaching a constant vector field) due to
\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

For
d\leq 2
