, the existence and uniqueness of global strong solution to (
1.5
) also hold if
\mathscr{D}
is a convex polyhedral domain. In this case, we still have the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
-elliptic regularity results (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), thus Proposition
3.1
,
3.3
, and
3.4
still hold in this setting to give the necessary
a priori
estimates for the existence of a global strong solution. Theorem
3.9
also holds in this case. For
d=3
, the
\mathbb{H}^{3}
-elliptic regularity result is used in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, which may not hold in a convex polyhedral domain. Higher order regularity estimates such as (
3.19
) also may not hold for a general convex domain.

4.
A linear fully discrete scheme for the LLB equation with spin-torques

In this section, we propose a linear fully discrete finite element scheme for the LLB equation with spin-torques. We begin by discussing some preliminary results on finite element approximation in the next subsection.

4.1.
Finite element approximation

Let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d=1,2,3
, be a smooth or convex polygonal (or convex polyhedral) domain. Let
\mathcal{T}_{h}
be a quasi-uniform triangulation of
\mathscr{D}

into intervals (in 1D), triangles (in 2D), or tetrahedra (in 3D)
with maximal mesh-size
h
.
To discretise the LLB equation, we
introduce the conforming finite element space
\mathbb{V}_{h}\subset\mathbb{H}^{1}
given by

(4.1)

\mathbb{V}_{h}:=\{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\in\boldsymbol{C}(\overline{\mathscr{D}};\mathbb{R}^{3}):\boldsymbol{\phi}|_{K}\in\cal{P}_{r}(K;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\;\forall K\in\cal{T}_{h}\},

where
\cal{P}_{r}(K;\mathbb{R}^{3})
denotes the space of polynomials of degree
r
on
K
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The case
r=1
(linear polynomials) or
r=2
(quadratic polynomials) are most commonly used.

As a consequence of the Bramble–Hilbert lemma
[
11
]
, for
p\in[1,\infty]
, there exists a constant
C
independent of
h
such that for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{W}^{r+1,p}
we have

(4.2)

\displaystyle\inf_{\chi\in{\mathbb{V}}_{h}}\left\{\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}+h\left\|{\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\chi})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\right\}\leq Ch^{r+1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{r+1,p}}.

Moreover, if the triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is quasi-uniform, we have the following inverse estimate: for
s\in\{0,1\}
and
1\leq q\leq p\leq\infty
,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{s,p}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{-d\left(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{s,q}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}.

In the analysis, we will use several projection and interpolation operators. The existence of such operators and the properties that they possess will be described below. Sufficient regularity conditions on the mesh will be assumed as needed so that the following stability and approximation properties hold.

Firstly, there exists an orthogonal projection operator
P_{h}:\mathbb{L}^{2}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\left\langle P_{h}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h},

with the following stability and approximation properties
[
11
,
16
,
19
]
: for any
p\in[1,\infty]
and
s\in\{0,1\}
, there exists a constant
C
independent of
h
such that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\left\|{P_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{s,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{s,p}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{W}^{s,p},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-P_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}+h\left\|{\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}-P_{h}\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r+1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{r+1,p}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{W}^{r+1,p}.

Furthermore, there exists a nodal (Lagrange) interpolation operator
\mathcal{I}_{h}:\mathcal{C}^{0}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
with these properties (
[
22
, Section 1.5]
and
[
55
]
): for any
p\in[1,\infty]
, there exists a constant
C
independent of
h
such that

(4.7)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,p}}

(4.9)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\mathcal{I}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}+h\left\|{\nabla\left(\boldsymbol{v}-\mathcal{I}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}\right)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r+1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{r+1,p}}.

Next, we introduce the discrete Laplacian operator
\Delta_{h}:\mathbb{V}_{h}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
defined by

(4.10)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{v}_{h},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle=-\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{h},\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}_{h},\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h},

as well as the Ritz projection
R_{h}:\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
defined by

(4.11)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla R_{h}\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle=0,\quad\text{such that}\quad\left\langle R_{h}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{1}\right\rangle=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}.

If
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, then we have
\Delta_{h}R_{h}\boldsymbol{v}=P_{h}\Delta\boldsymbol{v}
.
For any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
, let
\boldsymbol{\omega}:=\boldsymbol{v}-R_{h}\boldsymbol{v}
. The approximation property for the Ritz projection is assumed to hold
[
37
,
39
,
43
]
, namely for
s=0
or
1
and
p\in(1,\infty)
:

(4.12)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{s,p}}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\omega}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{s,p}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r+1-s}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{r+1,p}},

(4.13)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r+1}\left|{\ln h}\right|\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{r+1,\infty}}.

We also have the
\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}
stability property of the operator
R_{h}
, namely
[
18
,
38
,
45
]
:

(4.14)

\displaystyle\left\|{R_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}.

A condition which would guarantee all the stability and approximation properties listed above to hold is global quasi-uniformity of the triangulation (which we assumed for
\mathcal{T}_{h}
). However, they are also known to hold for mesh which is less regular (only locally quasi-uniform or mildly graded). We will not discuss these further, but instead refer interested readers to the references cited above.

Finally, the following inequalities hold: If
\mathscr{D}
is a convex polygonal or polyhedral domain with globally quasi-uniform triangulation, then there exists a constant
C
independent of
h
such that for any
\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

(4.15)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{4}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{d}{4}}+\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{d}{4}}\right),

(4.16)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{-2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Inequality (
4.15
) is shown in
[
30
, Appendix A]
), while (
4.16
) is shown in
[
8
]
.

4.2.
A linear fully discrete scheme

Before introducing a linear finite element scheme for solving (
1.5
), we need to define several multilinear forms that will facilitate the subsequent analysis.

Definition 4.1

(multilinear forms)
.

Given
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{L}^{\infty})
, and
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, we define the maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(\cdot\,,\,\cdot):\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\xi};\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot):\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot):\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\cdot\,,\,\cdot):\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{R},

by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle:=\alpha\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\xi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle:=\alpha\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi})\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle:=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle:=\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle.

If either
\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
or
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
, then by (
2.9
) and the divergence theorem, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{w})\right\rangle

(4.17)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=-\beta_{1}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}\left\langle(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle.

Furthermore, let

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}):=\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})+\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}).

Some properties of the multilinear forms defined above are gathered in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, and let the maps
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathcal{A}
be as defined in Definition
4.1
.

(i)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{A}_{1}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 3\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}},

(4.21)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\xi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

(4.22)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}.

(ii)

For all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
,
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v})=0
.

(iii)

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot)
is bounded, i.e. there exists a constant
\beta_{\mathrm{b}}>0
depending only on the coefficients of the equation,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}
, and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
, such that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})}\right|\leq\beta_{\mathrm{b}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

(iv)

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot)
is coercive, i.e.

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

Here,
\alpha
is a numerical coefficient in (
1.5a
).

(v)

If additionally
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{W}^{1,4}
and
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}},

(4.26)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\Delta\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

Proof.

Inequalities (
4.20
), (
4.21
), and (
4.22
) follow immediately by Hölder’s inequality. Statement (
ii
) is obvious since
\boldsymbol{a}\times\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
(\boldsymbol{a}\times\boldsymbol{b})\cdot\boldsymbol{b}=0
for any
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Inequality (
4.23
) follows from the estimates in part (
i
) and the triangle inequality.
Moreover, note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{|\boldsymbol{\phi}||\boldsymbol{v}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\geq\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

showing (
4.24
).

Next, we show (
4.25
) and (
4.26
). Firstly, integrating by parts the first term in the definition of
\mathcal{C}
, and noting the assumptions, we have

(4.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}\times\boldsymbol{v},\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}\times\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle-\beta_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle.

By Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\phi};\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{2}\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}},

where in the last step we used Sobolev embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, thus showing (
4.25
).
Similarly, inequality (
4.26
) follows from (
4.27
) with
\boldsymbol{v}=\Delta\boldsymbol{w}
and Hölder’s inequality, noting that the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.27
) now vanishes.
∎

In terms of the forms introduced in Definition
4.1
, the weak formulation (
2.8
) of the problem can be written as

(4.28)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\chi})=\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\chi}),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

Let
k
be the time step and
\mathbb{V}_{h}
be the finite element space (
4.1
). Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
be the approximation in
\mathbb{V}_{h}
of
\boldsymbol{u}(t)
at time
t=t_{n}=nk\in[0,T]
, where
n=0,1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor
. We denote
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}:=\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})
. For any discrete function
\boldsymbol{v}
, define for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{v}^{n}-\boldsymbol{v}^{n-1}}{k}.

We now describe a linear fully discrete scheme to solve (
4.28
). We start with
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}=P_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
. For
t_{n}\in[0,T]
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, given
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
, define
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
by

(4.29)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})=\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi}),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}.

To facilitate the proof of the error analysis, we split the approximation error as:

(4.30)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}=\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)+\left(\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)=:\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n},

where for any
t_{n}\in[0,T]
,
\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}=:\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})
is the elliptic projection of the solution
\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})
defined by

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n});\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}.

Note that scheme (
4.29
) and the elliptic projection are well-defined by the Lax–Milgram lemma.
We study some properties of this projection in the following section, while the error analysis is performed in Section
4.4
. For a technical reason (see Lemma
4.5
), we assume that in (
4.1
) the polynomial degree
r
in
\mathbb{V}_{h}
is:

(4.32)

\begin{cases}r\geq 1,&\text{if }d\in\{1,2\},\\
r\geq 2,&\text{if }d=3.\end{cases}

In this section, to attain an optimal order of convergence for the underlying approximation (
4.29
), we assume that the problem (
1.5
) admits a sufficiently regular solution
\boldsymbol{u}
which satisfies

(4.33)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{r+1})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{r+1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\leq K_{r},

for some positive constant
K_{r}
depending on the initial data,
r
, and
T
. Here,
r
is the degree of piecewise polynomials in the finite element space (
4.1
).

Note that the existence of a strong solution satisfying (
4.33
) for
r=1
is given by Theorem
3.8
, at least for a smooth domain (so the assumption (
4.33
) is reasonable). The existence of a more regular solution can be shown in a similar manner.

4.3.
Properties of elliptic projection

Relevant approximation and stability properties for the elliptic projection defined by (
4.31
) are derived in this section. We begin with the following lemma which will be needed to show some approximation properties of
\Pi_{h}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a smooth or a convex polyhedral domain. Assume that the exact solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of (
1.5
) belongs to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}})
. For any
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
and for each
t\in[0,T]
, there exists
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
such that

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))=\left\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

Moreover,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where the constant
C
depends on
\mathscr{D}
,
T
and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
.

Proof.

We use the Faedo–Galerkin method. Let
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
consisting of eigenfunctions for
-\Delta
such that

\displaystyle-\Delta\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\text{ in }\mathscr{D}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{e}_{i}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ on }\partial\mathscr{D},

where
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
are the eigenvalues of
-\Delta
associated with
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
.
Let
\mathbb{W}_{n}:=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}\}
.

Given
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
, for each
t\in[0,T]
, we approximate the solution to (
4.34
) by
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
satisfying

(4.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t))=\left\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}.

We need the following uniform bound:

(4.37)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Once this bound is established, we can apply the standard compactness argument: For each
t\in[0,T]
, the Banach–Alaoglu theorem and a compactness argument imply the existence of a subsequence, which is still denoted by
\{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t)\}
, such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t)\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\quad\text{weakly in }\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t)\to\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\quad\text{strongly in }\mathbb{W}^{1,4}.

A standard argument then shows
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)
satisfies (
4.34
), while (
4.35
) follows from (
4.37
).

It remains to prove (
4.37
). Setting
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}
in (
4.36
) and using the coercivity of
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n})=\left\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}\right\rangle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

which implies

(4.38)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Next, taking
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=-\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}
, integrating by parts as necessary, and applying (
4.21
) and (
4.26
) we have

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u};\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n})+\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u};\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n})-\left\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we also used the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, Sobolev embedding, and Young’s inequality. This, together with (
4.38
) and the elliptic regularity result, implies (
4.37
). The proof is now complete.
∎

With the above lemma, we can show the following approximation properties of
\Pi_{h}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}
be the elliptic projection of
\boldsymbol{u}
defined by (
4.31
),
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
1.5
) which satisfies (
4.33
), and
\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)=\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)
be as defined in (
4.30
). Then for any
t\in[0,T]
,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+h\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r+1},

(4.40)

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+h\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r+1},

where
r
is the degree of polynomials in the finite element space
\mathbb{V}_{h}
.
The constant
C
depends on
T
,
\mathscr{D}
,
K_{r}
, and the coefficients of the equation, where
K_{r}
was defined in (
4.33
).

Proof.

For all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
, by the boundedness and coercivity of
\mathcal{A}
in (
4.23
) and (
4.24
), as well as the definition of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{\eta}(t))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{\mathrm{b}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}},

since the first term in the second step is zero. Therefore, by (
4.2
),

(4.41)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq(\beta_{\mathrm{b}}/\alpha)\inf_{\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq Ch^{r}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{r+1})}.

To show the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-estimate, we use duality argument. For each
\boldsymbol{u}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
, let
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
satisfy

(4.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))=\left\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

For any
t\in[0,T]
, such
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)
exists by Lemma
4.3
under the assumed conditions on
\mathscr{D}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Therefore, taking
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)
in (
4.42
) and noting (
4.31
), we have for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)-\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{\mathrm{b}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\inf_{\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq Ch^{r+1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{r+1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where in the last step we used (
4.41
) and (
4.2
). This and (
4.41
) then implies inequality (
4.39
).

Next, we show (
4.40
). For ease of presentation, we omit the dependence of the functions on
t
. By the coercivity of
\mathcal{A}
and the definition of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
,

(4.43)

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta})=\mathcal{A}\left(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta})\right)+\mathcal{A}\left(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\right).

We will estimate each term on the last line. To this end, noting (
4.19
) and differentiating (
4.31
) with respect to
t
, we have for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

(4.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\chi})+2\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\chi})+\mathcal{C}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\chi})=0.

Thus, for the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.43
), by the boundedness of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}))\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|2\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}))\big|+\big|\mathcal{C}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}))\big|

(4.45)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq Ch^{r}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}},

where in the last step we also used (
4.39
) and (
4.8
). Here,
C
depends on
K_{r}
among other things (but is independent of
h
). For the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.43
), by the boundedness of
\mathcal{A}
and (
4.9
) we have

(4.46)

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})\big|\leq C\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq Ch^{r}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{r+1})}.

The estimates (
4.45
) and (
4.46
), together with (
4.43
) imply

(4.47)

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq Ch^{r},

where
C
depends on
K_{r}
, but is independent of
h
.
To estimate
\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, we use duality argument as before. For each
\boldsymbol{u}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
, let
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
satisfy

(4.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))=\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

The existence of
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)
is conferred by Lemma
4.3
, and furthermore we have

(4.49)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Taking
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)
in (
4.48
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}(t);\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))=\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

This equation and (
4.44
) yield for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi})+2\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\chi})+\mathcal{C}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u};\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi})+2\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi})-2\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{C}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{C}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u};\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi}).

We estimate the first four terms on the right-hand side above by using the boundedness of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{C}
(cf. (
4.23
), (
4.21
), and (
4.22
)). The fourth term is bounded using (
4.22
), while the last term is bounded using (
4.25
). Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}+2\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+2\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}+C\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}.

We now choose
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\mathcal{I}_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}
. Successively using (
4.47
), (
4.9
), and (
4.39
), noting the assumption (
4.33
) we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq(Ch^{r+1}+Ch^{r+3})\left\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\leq Ch^{r+1}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where in the last step we used (
4.49
) and the fact that
h<1
. Here,
C
depends on
K_{r}
(but is independent of
h
). This implies

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{r+1}.

This inequality and (
4.47
) together implies (
4.40
). The proof of this proposition will then be complete once we show the following regularity result in Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Next, we have the following result on the stability of the elliptic projection.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}
be the elliptic projection of
\boldsymbol{u}
, where
\boldsymbol{u}
is the solution of (
1.5
) satisfying the assumption (
4.33
). Suppose that the polynomial degree
r
in
\mathbb{V}_{h}
satisfies (
4.32
). Then for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C.

The constant
C
depends on
K_{r}
(defined in (
4.33
)) and
T
, but is independent of
h
.

Proof.

Let
P_{h}
be the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
projection defined in (
4.4
). By the triangle inequality, the inverse estimate (
4.3
), the stability and approximation properties (
4.8
) and (
4.9
), and (
4.39
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\mathcal{I}_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}+\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{-d/2}\big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}+\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))-\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\big)+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{-d/2}\big(Ch^{r}\big)+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{r-d/2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty})},

where
C
depends on
K_{r}
(defined in (
4.33
)), but is independent of
h
. Since
r\geq d/2
by assumption (
4.32
) and
h<1
, we obtain the required estimate.
∎

4.4.
Error analysis

We perform the error analysis for scheme (
4.29
) in this section. Recall that we split the error as a sum of two terms in (
4.30
). We also remark that for
p\in[1,\infty]

(4.50)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}=\left\|{\frac{1}{k}\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t_{n}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}.

First, we have the following stability estimate.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
T>0
be given and let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
be defined by (
4.29
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
. Then for any
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

(4.51)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{j-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{j}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where the constant
C
depends on
T
, but is independent of
n
and
k
.

Proof.

We set
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
in (
4.29
) and note the vector identity (
2.11
).
The required estimate then follows from similar argument as in
[
36
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

The following estimates on the nonlinear terms are needed later.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{D}
be as defined in Definition
4.1
, and suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}
is a solution of (
1.5
) that satisfies (
4.33
). Then there exists a constant
C
such that for any
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(4.52)

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2})+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

(4.53)

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

(4.54)

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(4.55)

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2})+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

The constant
C
depends on
\epsilon
, the coefficients of (
1.5
),
T
,
K_{r}
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
, or
k
).

Proof.

Firstly, note that we have

(4.56)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n-1}-k\cdot\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}.

Thus, by Hölder’s inequality, (
4.50
), (
4.39
), and the assumption (
4.33
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4/3}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4/3}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n-1}-k\cdot\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(4.57)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\right)(h^{r+1}+k).

We can then estimate

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\alpha\left\langle(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2})\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4/3}}\left\|{\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big[C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\right)(h^{r+1}+k)\Big]\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2})+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we used (
4.57
), Lemma
4.5
, and the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{4}
, while in the last step we used Young’s inequality and (
4.39
). This proves (
4.52
).

Next, noting (
4.17
) and (
4.56
), by Hölder’s inequality and Lemma
4.5
we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\times\nabla\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\big|\left\langle\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{e}\big(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\big|\beta_{2}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},\nabla(\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{\chi})\right\rangle+\big|\beta_{2}\left\langle\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n-1}-k\cdot\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where in the last step we used (
4.50
), (
4.39
), (
4.33
), and Young’s inequality. This proves (
4.53
).

Noting (
4.18
), (
4.33
), and (
4.56
), we have by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\big|\beta_{1}\left\langle(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle=C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n-1}-k\cdot\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

thus proving (
4.54
).

Finally, noting the definition of
\mathcal{A}
in (
4.19
), we infer inequality (
4.55
) from (
4.52
), (
4.53
), and the triangle inequality. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We can now prove an error estimate for scheme (
4.29
).

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}
be as defined in (
4.30
), where
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
solve (
4.29
) and (
4.28
), respectively. Then for
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2}),

where
C
depends on the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

Noting the definition of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}=\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
, the scheme (
4.29
), and the weak formulation (
2.8
), we have for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\right)-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

(4.58)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi})\right).

Now, we set
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}
in (
4.58
). Applying Lemma
4.7
and Proposition
4.4
, noting (
4.50
), the identity (
2.11
) and the coercivity of
\mathcal{A}
in (
4.24
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big|\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n})\big|+\big|\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|+\big|\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|+\big|\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n})-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n};\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2})+\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Summing over
m\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, using the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{4}
and noting (
4.51
), we infer

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2}\right)+Ck\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{m-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Note that since we take
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}=P_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}
, by (
4.6
) and (
4.39
),

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}-\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq\left\|{P_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}-\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}-\Pi_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{r+1}.

Thus, by the discrete Gronwall lemma (Lemma
2.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq C\left(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2}\right)\exp\left[Ck\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{m-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\right]\leq C\left(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2}\right),

where in the last step we again used (
4.51
). This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
4.29
) and (
4.28
), respectively. For
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left(k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{m}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(h^{r+1}+k),

where
C
depends on the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.8
and the triangle inequality (noting (
4.30
) and (
4.39
)).
∎

5.
An energy-stable implicit fully discrete scheme for the LLB equation

In the previous section, we proposed a linear scheme for the LLB equation (
1.5
) and proved its convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap\ell^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
. However, convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
could not be established analytically, primarily due to difficulties in deriving an energy stability estimate caused by nonlinear cross-product terms. Despite this, numerical results (see Section
6
) seem to suggest an optimal-rate convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
.

With the aim of obtaining a scheme with provable convergence in the energy norm, we consider in this section a fully implicit scheme for the LLB equation, restricted to the physically relevant case of
negligible non-adiabatic torque
(
\beta_{2}=0
). Even with this simplification, the equation remains a quasilinear vector-valued PDE. For the proposed scheme, we are able to show unconditional stability in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
, leading to a proof of unconditional convergence at an optimal rate in the same norm. As a corollary, in the absence of current (
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
), we show that this scheme also ensures energy dissipation (see (
1.8
)) at the discrete level.

Recall the weak formulation for the problem in this special case (
\beta_{2}=0
):

(5.1)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\boldsymbol{H}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

Here,
\boldsymbol{H}=\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}
, where
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{u}(t)+|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\boldsymbol{e}\big(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big)
as defined in (
2.3
), and
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is a given current density such that
\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
.
A fully discrete scheme to solve (
5.1
) can be described as follows. Let
\mathbb{V}_{h}
be the finite element space defined in (
4.1
) with
r\geq 1
. Let
k
be the time step and
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
be the approximation in
\mathbb{V}_{h}
of
\boldsymbol{u}(t)
at time
t=t_{n}:=nk\in[0,T]
for
n=0,1,2,\ldots,N
, where
N:=\lfloor T/k\rfloor
.
We start with
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}=R_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
for simplicity. For
t_{n}\in[0,T]
, given
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
, define
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
by

(5.2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h},

where

(5.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}+P_{h}\big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\big)+\boldsymbol{e}\left(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\right).

Throughout this section, for ease of presentation we assume
d=3
, noting that a similar (and simpler) argument will also hold for
d=1
or
2
.
Here, to attain an optimal order of convergence for scheme (
4.29
), we assume that the problem (
1.5
) admits a sufficiently regular solution
\boldsymbol{u}
which satisfies

(5.5)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{r+1})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{4})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{r+1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\leq K_{r},

for some positive constant
K_{r}
depending on the initial data,
r
, and
T
. Here,
r
is the degree of piecewise polynomials in the finite element space (
4.1
).

In the following lemma, we show the unconditional stability of (
5.2
) in the energy norm.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
T>0
be given and suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
satisfies (
5.2
). Then for any initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
and
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
, we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\rm{S}},

where
C_{\rm{S}}
depends only on the coefficients of the equation,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
, and
\mathscr{D}
. If
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then for any
k\in(0,1)
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n})\leq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}),

where
\mathcal{E}
is the energy functional defined in (
1.3
).

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}
be as defined in (
5.3
). Taking
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}
in (
5.2
), we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}\right\rangle

On the other hand, taking the inner product of (
5.3
) with
\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
, using vector identities (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n},\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2k}\big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2k}\big(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)-\frac{1}{4k}\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|^{2}-\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\frac{k}{2}\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|\left|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(5.9)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Substituting (
5.9
) into (
5.8
) and rearranging the terms yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2k}\big(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)+\frac{1}{4k}\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|^{2}-\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{k}{2}\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|\left|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(5.10)

\displaystyle=-\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}}{\alpha}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality. Rearranging the terms and applying discrete Gronwall’s lemma (Lemma
2.5
), we obtain (
5.6
).

Continuing from (
5.10
), if
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then the right-hand side of equation (
5.10
) is zero, which implies (
5.7
) upon rearranging the terms. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We also have the following discrete
\ell^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{\infty})
stability of the scheme.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
T>0
be given and let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
be defined by (
5.2
). Then for any initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
and
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

(5.11)

\displaystyle k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\Delta}.

Consequently, if
\mathscr{D}
is a convex polygonal or polyhedral domain with quasi-uniform triangulation, then

(5.12)

\displaystyle k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\leq C_{\infty},

where constants
C_{\Delta}
and
C_{\infty}
depend only on the coefficients of the equation,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
,
\mathscr{D}
, and
T
.

Proof.

Noting (
5.3
), by Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{3}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where in the last step we used the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
. Multiplying both sides by
k
, then summing over
m\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, and using (
5.6
) give (
5.11
). Applying (
4.15
) then yields (
5.12
).
∎

Note that scheme (
5.2
) is nonlinear. In the following lemma, we show that the scheme is well-posed.

Lemma 5.3
.

Given
k>0
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
, and current density
\boldsymbol{\nu}
such that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{L}^{\infty})}\leq\nu_{\infty}
, there exists
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
that solves scheme (
5.2
).

Proof.

Given
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
, let
\mathcal{A}_{n}:\mathbb{V}_{h}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
be a nonlinear operator defined by

\mathcal{A}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\boldsymbol{v}+P_{h}\big(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\boldsymbol{v}\big)+\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}).

Define a map
G_{h}:\mathbb{V}_{h}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
by

\displaystyle G_{h}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}+kP_{h}\big(\boldsymbol{v}\times(\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{v}-\mathcal{A}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}))\big)-k\alpha\big(\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{v}-\mathcal{A}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v})\big)-k\beta_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}.

The scheme (
5.2
) is equivalent to solving
G_{h}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\big)=\boldsymbol{0}
. The existence of solution to this equation will be shown using the Brouwer fixed point theorem. To this end, let
B_{\rho}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}:\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq\rho\}
. For any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\partial B_{\rho}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}:\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\rho\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle G_{h}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle+k\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\alpha\left\langle\mathcal{A}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle-k\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}

\displaystyle\quad+k\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-k\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+k\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}-k\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+k\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-k\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho^{2}-C_{\rm{S}}\right)+k\Big(\alpha\rho^{2}-\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty}C_{\rm{S}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho\Big).

Here,
C_{\rm{S}}
is the constant in the stability estimate (
5.6
). Therefore, for sufficiently large
\rho
, precisely

\rho>\max\Big\{C_{\rm{S}}^{\frac{1}{2}},\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty}C_{\rm{S}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\alpha^{-1}\Big\},

we have
\left\langle G_{h}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle>0
. Thus, we infer the existence of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
solving (
5.2
) by the Brouwer’s fixed point theorem.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

In fact if
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then
\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\big)
decays to zero exponentially fast as
t_{n}\to\infty
, mimicking the behaviour of the exact solution (see Theorem
3.9
) but possibly with different exponents. Indeed, taking
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
in (
5.2
) with
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, noting (
5.3
) and (
2.11
), gives

(5.13)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq 0.

Moreover, discarding non-negative terms in equation (
5.10
) with
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{2k}\big(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\big)

(5.14)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)+\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\leq 0.

Adding (
5.13
) and (
5.14
), noting the definition of
\mathcal{E}
in (
1.3
), then applying a version of the discrete Gronwall lemma
[
21
, equation (3.4)]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\big)\leq Ce^{-\beta t_{n}}\,\mathcal{E}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\big)

for some positive constants
C
and
\beta
.

Remark 5.5
.

Since scheme (
5.2
) is nonlinear, it is necessary to solve a system of nonlinear equations at each time step. Thus, a linearisation of the scheme is required. In Appendix
A
, we describe a simple fixed-point iteration for solving the resulting nonlinear system and establish its convergence. A detailed study of related topics, such as the efficiency of alternative iterative solvers (e.g., Newton’s method) and the discrete energy stability of the scheme when the nonlinear problem is solved approximately, will be the subject of future work. In the analysis that follows, we assume that the nonlinear system associated with scheme (
5.2
) is solved
exactly
at each time step (cf. Lemma
5.3
). Under this assumption, the stability estimates in stated in Lemma
5.1
and
5.2
hold.

Next, we will derive some estimates for the nonlinear terms to aid in the analysis, analogous to those in the previous section. To this end, we split the approximation error by writing

(5.15)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}=\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-R_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)+\left(R_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n},

where
R_{h}
is the Ritz projection defined in (
4.11
), while
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}
enjoys the estimates (
4.12
), (
4.13
), and (
4.14
).
We also remark that since
\Delta_{h}R_{h}\boldsymbol{v}=P_{h}\Delta\boldsymbol{v}
for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{N}}
, we have

(5.16)

\displaystyle\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}.

Noting (
5.3
), (
4.11
), and (
5.16
), we can write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{H}^{n}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n})-P_{h}\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)

(5.17)

\displaystyle\quad-(P_{h}-I)\left(|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)-\boldsymbol{e}\left(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n})\right),

where
I
is the identity operator. Moreover, we have

(5.18)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}

\displaystyle=\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right|^{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}\right)+\big((\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n})\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\boldsymbol{u}^{n}.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}
be defined by (
5.2
) and (
5.3
), respectively, with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
. Then there exists a constant
C
such that for all
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

(5.19)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(5.20)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Here,
C
depends on
\epsilon
, the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

Noting (
5.17
), we can write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\left(\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{H}^{n}\right)+\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}\right)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times P_{h}\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times(P_{h}-I)\left(|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\left(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n})\right)+\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}\right)\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n}

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{7}.

We will take the inner product of each term on the last line with
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}
and estimate it in the following. For the first term, by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, the stability estimate (
5.6
), as well as estimates (
4.12
) and (
2.17
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Similarly, for the terms
I_{3}
,
I_{6}
, and
I_{7}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{3},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{6},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{7},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the terms
I_{2}
and
I_{5}
, noting the regularity assumption (
5.5
), we use (
4.6
) and Young’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{2},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|+\left|{\left\langle I_{5},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{(P_{h}-I)\big(|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Finally, for the term
I_{4}
, we use (
5.18
), the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-stability of
P_{h}
, and stability estimate (
5.6
), to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{4},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{P_{h}\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+Ch^{r+1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we used (
4.12
), Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
, and Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality (
2.16
), while in the last step we used Young’s inequality.
Altogether, we obtain (
5.19
) from the above estimates.

In a similar manner, we obtain the following bounds:

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{1},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{2},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}h^{2(r+1)}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{3},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{5},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|
