\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{(P_{h}-I)\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{6},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{7},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Finally, for the term
\left\langle I_{4},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle
, we apply similar argument as in (
5.21
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{4},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{P_{h}\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+R_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we used the fact that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq C
and (
4.14
), while in the last step we used (
4.15
) and Young’s inequality.
Altogether, we obtain the inequality (
5.20
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

If
d=1
or
d=2
, then we have the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{8}
. In these cases, one could remove the term
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}
in (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) by estimating
\left|{\left\langle I_{4},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|
in (
5.21
) and
\left|{\left\langle I_{4},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|
in (
5.22
) differently. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{4},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\right)\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we also used (
4.12
). Similar bound also holds for
\left|{\left\langle I_{4},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|
. Furthermore, if we assume more regularity on the exact solution
\boldsymbol{u}
, say
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}^{4,3})
, then

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle I_{2},\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{2,3}}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

These stronger estimates allow us to avoid using (
4.14
), (
4.15
), and (
5.12
). Thus, we could remove the term
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}
in the estimates (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) if
d=1
or
2
, even without necessarily assuming global quasi-uniformity of the triangulation.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
be defined by (
5.2
). Then for any initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
and
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

(5.23)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

(5.24)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2r}+Ck^{2}+C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
C
depends on the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

Writing
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n-1}-k\cdot\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
, by (
2.6
) and Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|+\big|\left\langle(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n})(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n-1}-k\cdot\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality, (
4.50
), and (
4.12
), thus showing (
5.23
).

Next, by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities,

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n-1}-k\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2r}+Ck^{2}+C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

which shows (
5.24
).
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Let
\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{w}^{n}
be defined by (
5.4
) and (
2.3
), respectively.
Then for any initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
and
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

(5.25)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where
C
depends on the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}+P_{h}\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)+(P_{h}-I)\left(|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\right)+\boldsymbol{e}\left(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n})\right),

where
I
is the identity operator.
Noting (
4.6
), (
4.12
), and (
5.5
), we use the stability estimate (
5.6
) and (
5.18
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ch^{2(r+1)}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

This proves (
5.25
).
∎

We are now ready to prove an error estimate for the numerical scheme. In the proof, the following inequalities will be used:

(5.26)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\frac{1}{k}\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t_{n}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ch^{r+1}

(5.27)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\frac{1}{2k}\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t_{n}}(t-t_{n-1})\,\partial_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq Ck,

where (
4.12
) was used in the last step of (
5.26
), while Taylor’s theorem and (
5.5
) were used in (
5.27
).
We begin by showing an auxiliary error estimate. In case
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, the following proposition shows a superconvergence estimate for
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}
be as defined in (
5.15
). Then for
n\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

(5.28)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(h^{2r}+k^{2}).

If
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then we have a superconvergence estimate

(5.29)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2}).

In particular, if
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with globally quasi-uniform triangulation, then

(5.30)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\leq C\big(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2}\big)\left|{\ln h}\right|,

where
C
depends on the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

Subtracting (
5.1
) from (
5.2
), then applying (
5.16
) and (
2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}+(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle.

Now, we put
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}
and rearrange the terms (noting the identity (
2.11
)) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha\left\langle(P_{h}-I)\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle-\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}),\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}\right\rangle

(5.31)

\displaystyle=:J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{5}.

It remains to estimate each term on the last line. For the term
J_{1}
, by (
5.26
) and (
5.27
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\left\|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we also used Young’s inequality.
For the next term, we apply Lemma
5.6
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)h^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{3}
, by Young’s inequality and (
4.6
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{3}}\right|\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{4}
, we use (
5.25
) and Young’s inequality to infer

\displaystyle\left|{J_{4}}\right|\leq Ch^{2(r+1)}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the last term, by Lemma
5.8
. we obtain

(5.32)

\displaystyle\left|{J_{5}}\right|\leq Ch^{2r}+Ck^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, substituting these estimates into (
5.31
), after rearranging the terms we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(5.33)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)h^{2r}+Ck^{2}+C_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

for some positive constant
C_{1}
which depends on the coefficients of (
1.5
) (that we do not specify in detail, but can be traced from the preceding calculations). Therefore, summing (
5.33
) over
m\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
and noting (
5.12
) yield

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\alpha k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C(1+C_{\infty})h^{2r}+Ck^{2}+C_{1}k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{m}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

For sufficiently small
k
such that
C_{1}k<1
, we obtain inequality (
5.28
) for the first two terms on the left-hand side by the generalised discrete Gronwall lemma (Lemma
2.6
). For the last term in (
5.28
), the estimate follows by applying (
4.15
).

Next, if
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then the term
J_{5}
is absent in (
5.31
), thus estimate (
5.32
) is not needed. Instead of (
5.33
), we then have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2k}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n-1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)h^{2(r+1)}+Ck^{2}+C_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

from which (
5.29
) follows by similar argument as before.

Finally, inequality (
5.30
) follows from (
5.29
) and the discrete Sobolev inequality
[
10
]
.
∎

We have the following main theorem for this section, which shows convergence at optimal rates in various norms.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
5.2
) and (
5.1
), respectively. For
n\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor T/k\rfloor\}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq C(h^{r}+k).

If
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then for
s\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C(h^{r+1-s}+k),

\displaystyle k\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(h^{2(r+1)}+k^{2})\left|{\ln h}\right|.

If
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with globally quasi-uniform triangulation, then

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq C\big(h^{r+1}+k\big)\left|{\ln h}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
C
depends on the coefficients of the equation,
K_{r}
,
T
, and
\mathscr{D}
(but is independent of
n
,
h
or
k
).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.10
and the triangle inequality (noting (
5.15
), (
4.12
), and (
4.13
)).
∎

6.
Numerical experiments

Numerical simulations for schemes (
4.29
) and (
5.2
) are performed using the open-source package
FEniCS

[
3
]
. In these simulations, we take the domain
\mathscr{D}=[-0.5,0.5]^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and use
\mathcal{P}_{1}
Lagrange finite element space (corresponding to
r=1
in (
4.1
)). Since the exact solution of the equation is not known, we use extrapolation to verify the spatial order of convergence experimentally. To this end, let
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
be the finite element solution with spatial step size
h
and time step size
k=\lfloor T/n\rfloor
. For
s\in\{0,1\}
, define the extrapolated order of convergence

\text{rate}_{s}:=\log_{2}\left[\frac{\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{2h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}{\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{h}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}\right],

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{h}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h/2}^{n}
. We expect that for both schemes, when
k
is sufficiently small,
\text{rate}_{s}\approx h^{2-s}
. To test for the temporal rate of convergence in the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
norm, we set
k=Ch^{2}
in the simulation and check that
\text{rate}_{s}\approx h^{2}
. Similarly, to test for the temporal rate of convergence in the
\mathbb{H}^{1}
norm, we set
k=Ch
in the simulation and check that
\text{rate}_{s}\approx h
. In each experiment, we plot the graph of energy vs time to show the energy evolution of the system for both numerical schemes.

6.1.
Simulation 1 (small current density)

The coefficients in (
1.5
) are
\gamma=2.2\times 10^{5},\alpha=1.0\times 10^{5},\beta_{1}=0.1,\beta_{2}=-0.01,\sigma=1.3\times 10^{-6},\kappa=1.0,\mu=1.0\times 10^{-6}
, and
\lambda=1.0\times 10^{-3}
, which are of typical order of magnitude for a micromagnetic simulation. The anisotropy axis
\boldsymbol{e}=(0,1,0)^{\top}
. We choose the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, as in
[
8
,
9
]
, to be

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y)=\left(2x(1-2|\boldsymbol{x}|)^{4},2y(1-2|\boldsymbol{x}|)^{4},\frac{(1-2|\boldsymbol{x}|)^{8}-|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2}}{(1-2|\boldsymbol{x}|)^{8}+|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2}}\right),

where
|\boldsymbol{x}|:=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
.
Numerical simulations are performed for schemes (
4.29
) and (
5.2
) with time step size
k=1.0\times 10^{-6}
and a small current density
\boldsymbol{\nu}=(10^{4},0)^{\top}
.

Although we establish convergence of the nonlinear scheme (
5.2
) only when
\beta_{2}=0
, both linear and nonlinear schemes are employed in this simulation. Snapshots of the magnetic spin field
\boldsymbol{u}
at selected times are presented in Figure
1
for the linear scheme and in Figure
2
for the nonlinear scheme. The two figures exhibit close qualitative agreement, although the linear scheme achieves this with a shorter runtime. The colour scale represents the relative magnitude of the magnetisation vectors. In both simulations, a central cluster of magnetisation vectors aligned in the
+z
direction is advected to the right by the current. This structure remains relatively stable even at elevated temperatures, illustrating its robustness and manipulability via current. After this structure exits the ferromagnetic domain, the current continues to drive a spin wave
[
46
]
, which is visible as it propagates through the magnetic medium.

\boldsymbol{e}_{h}
against
1/h
are shown in Figures

6.3.
Simulation 3 (adiabatic torque only)

We consider scheme (
5.2
) with positive
\mu
and take
k=1.0\times 10^{-6}
. The coefficients in (
1.5
) are
\gamma=2.3\times 10^{5},\alpha=2.0\times 10^{5},\sigma=1.0\times 10^{-6},\kappa=2.0,\mu=2.0\times 10^{-6}
,
\lambda=0.01
,
\beta_{1}=0.2
, and
\beta_{2}=0
. The anisotropy axis vector is
\boldsymbol{e}=(0,0,1)^{\top}
and the current density is
\boldsymbol{\nu}=(0,10^{4})^{\top}
. The initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
is given by the vortex state

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y)=\big(-y,x,0\big).

Snapshots of the magnetic spin field
\boldsymbol{u}
at selected times are shown in Figure
11
for the linear scheme and in Figure
12
for the nonlinear scheme. The two results exhibit good qualitative agreement, although the linear scheme appears to be computationally more efficient. The colour scale represents the relative magnitude of the magnetisation vectors. A vortex-like microstructure, which is widely explored as a potential means of data storage
[
26
]
, remains stable even as the object undergoes demagnetisation at temperatures exceeding the Curie temperature. The vortex is advected in the
+y
-direction by the current, suggesting that electric current may be used to transport such vortex within the magnet even at high temperatures. Following this motion, spin waves are observed propagating through the ferromagnet.

\boldsymbol{e}_{h}
against
1/h
are shown in Figures

and

\boldsymbol{e}_{h}

with

and

. Graphs of energy vs time are plotted in Figures

and

6.4.
Simulation 4 (energy dissipation)

In this last simulation, we consider an academic example where no current and no anisotropy is present. For this numerical experiment, scheme (
5.2
) is seen to preserve energy dissipation at the discrete level, while scheme (
4.29
) does not. The coefficients in (
1.5
) are taken to be
\gamma=2.5\times 10^{12}
,
\alpha=0.2
,
\sigma=1.0\times 10^{-10}
,
\kappa=0.1
,
\mu=1.0\times 10^{-7}
, and
\lambda=0
. Since no current is assumed,
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
We take
k=1\times 10^{-5}
and let the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
be given by

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y)=\big(-y,x,0.01\big).

Graphs of energy vs time are plotted in Figures
16(a)
and
16(b)
for the two schemes. Physical theory indicates that energy should be dissipated by the system, and this is reflected at the discrete level for scheme
5.2
in Figure
16(b)
. However, as seen in Figure
16(a)
, this is not maintained at the discrete level for scheme
4.29
.

(a)

Plot of energy vs time for the
linear
scheme (
4.29
) in Simulation 4.

(b)

Plot of energy vs time for the
nonlinear
scheme (
5.2
) in Simulation 4.

7.
Conclusion

In this paper, we show the unique existence of global strong (and more regular) solution to the LLB equation with spin-torques above the Curie temperature on
\mathscr{D}\subset{R}^{d}
, where
d\leq 3
(with an additional small data assumption if
d=3
). Certain smoothing and decay estimates are also given. We then propose two finite element schemes to approximate the solution: a linear scheme
4.29
and a nonlinear scheme
5.2
. We show that the first (linear) scheme is stable and convergent (with an optimal order) in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;{L}^{2})\cap\ell^{2}(0,T;{H}^{1})
, but unfortunately we are not able to show stability and convergence for this scheme in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;{H}^{1})
. Furthermore, while it is linear, this scheme may not maintain the energy dissipation property of the system in the absence of current at the discrete level. On the other hand, the second scheme, albeit being nonlinear, is provably stable and convergent in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;{H}^{1})
, but we can only show this when the non-adiabatic torque is omitted (which is actually physically justifiable in most standard cases). Furthermore, in the absence of current, this second scheme is able to maintain energy dissipativity at the discrete level.

This begs the question: Which scheme is the ‘best’? At this point, we concede that each scheme has its own advantages and shortcomings. In general, when a current is present, the linear scheme exhibits shorter runtimes while producing results that are qualitatively similar to those of the nonlinear scheme (see Simulation 1, 2, and 3 in Section
6
). In the absence of current, when simulating processes that require more precise knowledge of the energy relaxation behaviour – such as laser- or heat-induced (de)magnetisation dynamics
[
5
]
– the nonlinear energy-dissipative scheme (
5.2
) may be preferred (cf. Simulation 4).

Altogether, these results improve upon the existing theoretical and numerical findings on the LLB equation in the literature
[
7
,
9
,
35
,
36
,
47
]
, and further extend them to incorporate spin-torque effects. The general LLB equation (
1.1
) will be addressed in a forthcoming paper. As for future work, we aim to develop a numerical scheme for the LLB equation that is both linear and energy-dissipative in the absence of current. It would also be of interest to rigorously establish convergence of this linear scheme (
4.29
) in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;{H}^{1})
, as suggested by the numerical experiments.

Appendix A
A fixed-point iteration for the nonlinear scheme

At each time step in scheme (
5.2
), a system of nonlinear equations must be solved. To address this, we propose a simple fixed-point iteration, outlined in the following algorithm, which also demonstrates the uniqueness of the finite element solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}
under certain technical conditions.

Algorithm A.1
.

Input: initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}
, current
\{\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\}_{0\leq n\leq N}
, and tolerance
\mathsf{tol}>0
.

For
n=1,2,\ldots,N
, iterate:

(i)

Set
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,0}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}
.

For
j\in{N}
:

(1)

Compute
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\in{V}_{h}
such that for all
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in{V}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\alpha\left\langle\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\alpha\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

(A.1)

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\alpha\left\langle\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle,

where
\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}:=\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}-P_{h}\big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}\big)
;

(2)

If
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\geq\mathsf{tol}
, then replace
j\mapsto j+1
and go back to (
1
);

(3)

Otherwise if
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}<\mathsf{tol}
, then set
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}
, replace
n\mapsto n+1
, and return to (
i
).

Output: a sequence of discrete functions
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\}_{1\leq n\leq N}
.

Note that step (
A.1
) is well-posed. To see this, for a given
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}\in{V}_{h}
, we define a bilinear form
a:{V}_{h}\times{V}_{h}\to{R}
by

\displaystyle a(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi})

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle+k\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle-\alpha k\left\langle\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle+\alpha k\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}|^{2}\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle+\alpha k\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle+\alpha k\left\langle\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle

and a linear form
L:{V}_{h}\to{R}
by

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\phi})

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle+\beta_{1}k\left\langle(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle.

Then (
A.1
) is equivalent to solving

(A.2)

\displaystyle a(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi})=L(\boldsymbol{\chi}),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in{V}_{h}.

Note that the bilinear form
a
is bounded and
{V}_{h}
-elliptic, namely for any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\in{V}_{h}
,

\displaystyle|a(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi})|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+k\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}+\alpha k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}+2\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}},

and

\displaystyle a(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}||\boldsymbol{v}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\geq\min\left(1,\alpha k\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}.

For the linear form
L
we have

\displaystyle\left|{L(\boldsymbol{\phi})}\right|\leq\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty}k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

Hence, we deduce that (
A.1
) admits a unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\in{V}_{h}
by the Lax–Milgram lemma. The following proposition shows that under certain conditions, Algorithm
A.1
is guaranteed to be well-posed.

Proposition A.2
.

If
k=o(h^{\beta})
, where
\beta:=\max\left(2+\frac{d}{2},\frac{3d}{2}\right)
, then there exists a unique sequence
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\}_{1\leq n\leq N}
solving Algorithm
A.1
.

Proof.

Observe that step (
A.1
) is well-defined by the argument preceding this proposition. We start with some preliminary estimates. Firstly, for a given
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}
, we put
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}
in (
A.2
) and use (
2.6
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}k\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\right\rangle-\beta_{1}k\left\langle(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{n})\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha k}{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha k}{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{2k(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}}{\alpha}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality and the assumption that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathscr{D};\,{R}^{d}))}\leq\nu_{\infty}
.
This implies, after rearranging the terms,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\left(1+4k(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}\alpha^{-1}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Next, subtraction of equation (
A.1
) for two consecutive iterations yields

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-k\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle+\alpha k\left\langle\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha k\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle-\alpha k\left\langle\boldsymbol{e}\big(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j})\big),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle

Putting
\boldsymbol{\chi}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}
and rearranging the terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j})}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-k\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\times(\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}),\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}\right\rangle-\alpha k\left\langle\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}|^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j},\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq k\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

(A.4)

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}+\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

Note that we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}\right)-\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-P_{h}\left(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|^{2}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1})\right)-P_{h}\left(\big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}|^{2}\big)\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}\right).

Therefore, by Hölder’s inequality and inverse estimates ((
4.3
) and (
4.16
)), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{-2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+Ch^{-d}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

Continuing from (
A.4
), using this inequality and inverse estimates, we then have

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j})}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\alpha k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq k\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{H}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha k\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}+\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ckh^{-\left(2+\frac{d}{2}\right)}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ckh^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ckh^{-\frac{3d}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha k}{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}||\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha kh^{-d}}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}+\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Rearranging the terms and using (
A.3
), we infer

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ckh^{-\left(2+\frac{d}{2}\right)}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ckh^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ckh^{-\frac{3d}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ckh^{-d}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}+\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\leq Ckh^{-\beta}\left(1+4k(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}\alpha^{-1}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{3}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
\beta=\max\left(2+\frac{d}{2},\frac{3d}{2}\right)
, and in the last step we used (
A.3
).

We now proceed to prove the proposition inductively. Given that Algorithm
A.1
has produced an output
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\ell}\}_{0\leq\ell\leq n-1}
, we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\ell}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\ell,j_{\ell}}
for some
j_{\ell}
, where
\ell=1,2,\ldots,n-1
. Thus, by repeatedly applying (
A.3
), for
\ell=1,2,\ldots,n-1
we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\ell}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\ell,j_{\ell}}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\left(1+4k(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}\alpha^{-1}\right)^{n-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\exp\left(4T(\beta_{1}\nu_{\infty})^{2}\alpha^{-1}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{0}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

This inequality and (
A.5
) imply that

(A.6)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ckh^{-\beta}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n,j-1}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
C
is a constant which depends only on the coefficients of the equation, the initial data, and
T
. By the Banach fixed-point theorem, if
k=o(h^{\beta})
as
h,k\to 0^{+}
, then the contraction property (
A.6
) implies the existence of a unique
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{n}\in{V}_{h}
solving Algorithm
A.1
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark A.3
.

The condition
k=o(h^{\beta})
in Proposition
A.2
is restrictive and appears to arise primarily due to the techniques employed in our analysis. In practical computations, however, this condition seems overly conservative. Although we are currently unable to establish energy dissipativity of the fully discrete scheme when the nonlinear system is solved approximately at each time step using Algorithm
A.1
, as opposed to being solved exactly, our numerical experiments seem to suggest that the scheme retains a form of energy dissipation (in the absence of current) even under inexact solver at each time step. This issue merits further investigation.
